The largest proportional burdens fall on a different

group of countries. Cyprus has the strongest estimated
growth impact at 1.74pp, followed by Belgium at 1.41pp,
Italy at 1.19pp, Croatia at 1.08pp and Greece at 1.03pp.
For Cyprus, this translates into approximately EUR0.7bn,
or 1.7% of projected annual GDP. Germany’s much larger
monetary loss corresponds to around 0.53% of GDP.
The results also show why air temperature alone is not
sufficient metric to economic heat exposure. During
Italy’s selected period, the population-weighted air

temperature averages 30.8°C and the dewpoint 21.5°C.
Hungary is considerably hotter at 34.6°C, but its dewpoint
is lower at 17.0°C. Their corresponding mean daily
maximum wet-bulb temperatures are 24.1°C and 22.6°C,
respectively, and the model estimates a larger growth
loss for Italy: 1.19pp against 0.51pp in Hungary. Humidity
helps explain why some countries with less striking air
temperatures nevertheless face substantial heat stress.
This interpretation is consistent with firm-level evidence

across 23 advanced economies: Costa et al. (2024) find
that high humidity and longer heatwaves amplify the
adverse effects of heat on labor productivity¹¹.
Heat can reduce output without causing visible
damage to buildings or equipment.  Workers may
work more slowly, require longer breaks or miss work
altogether. Evidence from Indian manufacturing links
hot conditions to both lower worker productivity and
increased absenteeism, while finding that climate control

mitigates productivity losses. These findings help explain
how heat exposure can translate into lost production,
although they do not establish the size of the European
losses estimated here. The national results also conceal
differences between workplaces: population weighting
brings the calibration closer to where people experience
heat, but does not directly measure occupational
exposure, access to cooling or firms’ capacity to
reorganize work¹².

### Page 20

20
The 2027 climate outlook
2027 is likely to be another year of exceptional global
heat and has a credible chance of becoming the warmest
year on record. The WMO projects annual global mean
near-surface temperatures at between 1.3°C and 1.9°C
above the 1850–1900 average during 2026–2030¹³.  It
estimates an 86% probability that at least one of these
years will surpass 2024 as the warmest recorded, a 91%
probability of another annual exceedance of 1.5°C and a

75% probability that the five-year mean will exceed that
level. No final ranking can yet be assigned to 2026, and the
WMO does not provide a separate probability for 2027.
However, its lead author specifically identifies 2027 as a
potential record year because El Niño is expected to peak
towards the end of 2026. A June 2026 research places
annual global surface air temperature (GSAT) close to
1.7°C in 2027¹⁴.
El Niño will add a temporary boost to temperatures

in 2027, rather than explain the underlying warming
trend. The forecast strengthened markedly during 2026.
The WMO’s May outlook indicated a tendency towards
El Niño, particularly in 2027 and 2028; by September,
the event was firmly established and considered almost
certain to persist through February 2027. This was the
first time a WMO El Niño/La Niña Update had been so
unequivocal about the persistence of an event. The Niño
3.4 anomaly averaged 1.5°C during May–July and had

risen to between 2.2°C and 2.6°C by mid-August, while
subsurface anomalies exceeded 8°C in places. The WMO
projects a Niño 3.4 anomaly of around 3.6 ± 0.4°C for
September–November, followed by a peak towards the
end of 2026¹⁵.  Since the global temperature response
can extend beyond the peak in Pacific warming, part of
the additional effect is likely to carry into 2027¹⁶ ¹⁷.
The weather outlook combines a widespread heat
signal with much less uniform rainfall risks. The

latest WMO seasonal forecast favors above-normal
temperatures across almost all land areas entering 2027,
including strong signals over most of Australia, southern
Africa and large parts of South America. Rainfall
patterns are more divided. Drier conditions are favored
over the Indian subcontinent, southern Central America
and the Caribbean, and northwestern and northern
coastal South America; wetter conditions are favored
across parts of North America, the Greater Horn of Africa

and southeastern South America. The rainfall signal
over Australia remains weak. These forecasts describe
the conditions leading into 2027, not the whole year.
Moreover, the strength of El Niño does not determine
the severity of impacts in each country, which will also
depend on the timing of the event and on conditions in
the Indian and Atlantic Oceans.

### Page 21

24 September 2026
21
A record-hot 2027 would represent another annual
exceedance of 1.5°C, but would not by itself establish
a breach of the Paris temperature goal. Applying their
published extrapolation method, Kirchengast and Pichler
(2025)¹⁸  place the 20-year warming level centered on
2024 at 1.39 [1.29–1.49]°C GSAT. The June 2026 research
extends this estimate to 1.55 ± 0.10°C GSAT for the center
year 2027. Both figures are observation-constrained

and projection-assisted, with projections accounting
for a larger part of the 2027 window. Dividing the latter
estimate by the authors’ 1.06 GMST-to-GSAT factor
gives approximately 1.46°C in global mean surface
temperature. This is broadly consistent with Foster and
Rahmstorf’s (2026)¹⁹  finding that, if the recent GMST
trend continues, the long-term 1.5°C level will be reached
before 2030. El Niño may therefore determine whether
2027 sets a new annual record; emissions will determine

whether warming subsequently settles above the Paris
threshold.
El Niño is likely to have small to moderate
economic effects in 2027, but their direction and
scale vary markedly across countries (Figure 10a).
Most economies experience lower output as higher
temperatures weigh on productivity and activity. A
smaller group sees relatively cooler conditions than
in the baseline, modestly supporting growth. This is
compatible with an exceptionally warm year globally:

the temperature response to El Niño is geographically
uneven, and individual countries can move in opposite
directions around the rising global trend.
The estimates use the 2023–2024 super El Niño to
calibrate the country-level temperature response.
For each country, we use the change in temperature
recorded during that episode to construct the
corresponding 2027 shock. This is then translated into
effects on GDP growth, headline inflation and food

inflation. The economic baseline combines 2026 real
GDP with 2027 growth assumptions from the IMF
World Economic Outlook, supplemented by World
Bank forecasts where necessary. Monetary effects are
expressed in constant 2021 PPP-adjusted USD. The
exercise should be interpreted as a temperature-channel
scenario rather than a precise country forecast, since
rainfall, crop losses and physical damage could alter the
eventual outcome. Figure 10a presents a selection of

countries, while Appendix A6 reports the full results.
The global monetary cost is driven as much by the
size of the affected economies as by the severity of
their individual shocks.  Among the 144 countries with
complete GDP data, 109 record lower output, 34 record
gains and one is broadly unchanged. Gross losses reach
an estimated USD488bn, partly offset by USD37bn of
gains, leaving a net reduction of around USD451bn,
or 0.24% of their combined projected GDP in 2027.

China records the largest monetary loss, at USD147bn,
with GDP 0.38% below baseline. It is followed by the
US at USD91bn, or 0.33%, and India at USD50bn, or
0.30%. Together, these three economies account for
almost 60% of the gross loss. The proportional effects
are larger in South Korea and Indonesia, at 0.51% and
0.41% of baseline GDP , respectively, although their
monetary losses are lower at USD15bn and USD19bn.
France, Argentina, Nigeria and the UK are among the

larger economies on the other side of the distribution,
with estimated gains ranging from USD2bn to USD7bn.
The EU-27 aggregate conceals a marked divide
between countries. Output falls below baseline in 21
member states, generating gross losses of USD37.3bn.
Gains of USD13.3bn in the other six reduce the net
EU-27 cost to around USD24bn, equivalent to 0.09% of
projected GDP. Italy, Poland and Germany record the
largest monetary losses, at approximately USD7.0bn,

USD6.7bn and USD6.3bn, respectively. Greece
experiences the largest proportional decline among the
EU countries shown, at 0.55%, but its smaller economy
limits the monetary loss to USD2.3bn. France provides
the largest offset, with a gain of USD7.3bn, followed
by Spain at USD2.7bn and Ireland at USD2.4bn. The
relatively small EU-wide effect therefore reflects
offsetting national outcomes rather than a uniformly
limited exposure.
The inflationary effects are broader than the output

effects and are generally stronger for food prices
(Figure 10b). Across the countries with complete
GDP data, a PPP-GDP-weighted summary points
to an increase of 0.42pp in headline inflation and
0.65pp in food inflation. The corresponding EU-27
effects are 0.31pp and 0.50pp. The largest increases
are concentrated in Northern and Eastern Europe.
Headline inflation rises by around 1.5pp in Estonia,
Latvia, Sweden and Poland, while food inflation
increases by between 2.35pp and 2.57pp. Among the

18 A traceable global warming record and clarity for the 1.5 °C and well-below-2 °C goals | Communications Earth & Environment
19  Global Warming Has Accelerated Significantly - Foster - 2026 - Geophysical Research Letters - Wiley Online Library

### Page 22

Allianz Research
22
major economies, South Korea records increases of
1.02pp in headline inflation and 1.59pp in food inflation.
The corresponding effects reach 0.67pp and 1.04pp
in China, 0.57pp and 0.84pp in India and 0.18pp and
0.29pp in the US. Countries receiving a more favorable
temperature impulse generally move in the opposite
direction: inflation declines in Ireland, Argentina, Spain,
France and the UK. The stronger response of food prices

means that the household effect could be more visible
than the relatively moderate GDP percentages suggest,
particularly in economies where food represents a large
share of consumption.
These results should be read as a sensitivity analysis,
not as a forecast of the realized economic effects of the
2026–2027 El Niño. The exercise assumes that country-
level temperature responses broadly resemble those
observed during the 2023–2024 episode. This pattern

could change with the strength, timing and geographical
center of the event, as well as its interaction with the Indian
Ocean Dipole, Atlantic sea-surface temperatures and
regional atmospheric and soil-moisture conditions. The
estimates pass the resulting temperature shocks through
temperature-driven damage functions and therefore
do not capture additional losses from discrete extreme
events. These could include severe heatwave in southern
and western Europe, flooding and landslides in Peru and

Ecuador, drought and agricultural losses in Indonesia
and southern Africa, wildfire conditions across parts of
Southeast Asia, or changes in tropical-cyclone activity in
the central and eastern Pacific. If such events materialize,
the eventual economic cost could be substantially higher
than the temperature-based estimates reported here.

Adaptation responses to major
climate events in 2025–2026
Recent climate events provide a practical test of how
countries are adapting to rising physical risks.  This
section examines major weather and climate events
recorded in 2025 and 2026 across Europe, Latin and
Central America, North America, Asia-Pacific, Sub-Saharan
Africa, and the Middle East and North Africa. Events were
first screened for record or near-record severity in at least

one material dimension, including hazard intensity, area
affected, human consequences, economic or insured
losses, and disruption to critical infrastructure. The final
selection captures distinct regional patterns while limiting
overlap between events with similar risk profiles.
For each event, we examine how the affected countries
responded and whether the experience led to broader
adaptation measures.  The comparison covers the main
adaptation priorities, measures already implemented or

announced, and the funding committed or still required.
It also distinguishes immediate emergency and recovery
actions from longer-term efforts to reduce exposure
and vulnerability. The aim is to identify where recent
shocks have accelerated preparedness and investment,
and where adaptation remains fragmented, reactive or
insufficiently funded.
Europe
Europe is experiencing rapidly changing climate
conditions, making previously rare extremes
increasingly common. Preparedness, however, varies

across countries and hazards. The events of 2025–2026
illustrate this shift across several hazards: extreme
heat has become a recurrent threat across much of the
continent, while persistent drought and water scarcity
have affected both southern Europe and major river
basins further north (Table 1). Wildfire activity reached
unprecedented levels in the EU in 2025 and remained
a major concern in the Mediterranean in 2026.  Severe
storms and flooding also continued to cause substantial

impacts, particularly across southern and western
Europe. The scale of these events is prompting new
adaptation measures, including stronger EU wildfire
preparedness, expanded heat health planning and new
water resilience measures. Together, these developments
highlight the need to strengthen adaptation not only
where hazards are already well established, but also in
regions where changing conditions are creating new or
more frequent risks.

Adaptation priorities differ across Europe, reflecting
differences in exposure, vulnerability and existing
preparedness. Europe has substantial capacity for
emergency response, early warning and post-disaster
support, but adaptation remains uneven and greater
emphasis is needed on preventing and reducing risks

before events occur. This is particularly important as
hazards increasingly affect countries and regions with
less experience of them: heat resilience needs to be
integrated into building renovation, urban planning and
health systems, while flood risk needs to be addressed
through both infrastructure and more risk-sensitive
spatial planning. In southern and increasingly water-
stressed parts of Europe, improving water efficiency,
reuse and natural water retention will be central to

managing drought risk. Meanwhile, in wildfire-prone
areas, prevention through landscape management and
community preparedness needs to complement growing
firefighting capacity. A further European challenge is
the significant gap between rising climate-related losses
and available financial protection. Closing this insurance
protection gap, alongside more systematic risk-informed
planning and investment in prevention, will be important
to reduce the growing costs of climate impacts.

Source: Allianz Research

### Page 25

24 September 2026
25
Asia Pacific
Asia Pacific shows clear differences in adaptation
maturity, with countries varying in how far they have
moved from emergency response towards established
prevention systems (Table 2). Australia presents
the most developed framework: bushfire prediction,
building standards, community resilience and dedicated
funding are already embedded in national and local
institutions, although implementation still varies by
region. Japan also has strong emergency capacity, as

shown by the extensive response to the Ofunato wildfire,
but the balance remains tilted towards firefighting,
evacuation and recovery rather than reducing forest-
fire risk in advance. China occupies an intermediate
position. Its response to Typhoon Ragasa combined
forecasting technology, regional data sharing and
rapid infrastructure restoration with clearly identified
emergency funding. Cooperation around the Nepal–
China floods also improved early warning, but lacked a

dedicated adaptation budget. Indonesia has expanded
hotspot monitoring, weather modification and local
coordination, yet persistent land-management problems
and the absence of a separate wildfire budget limit the
shift from suppression to prevention. India ranks lower
in terms of institutional consolidation: heat adaptation
draws on building projects, welfare measures and
climate-finance platforms, but delivery remains
dispersed across schemes and jurisdictions. The regional

pattern suggests that repeated exposure can strengthen
operational capacity, while long-term adaptation
advances more slowly when responsibilities and budgets
are divided among several levels of government.

Central and South America
Across Central and South America, adaptation
capacity is shaped less by the volume of resources
mobilized than their timing, continuity and purpose
(Table 3). Honduras and Chile represent the strongest
approaches, although through different models:
Honduras links funding to forecasts so that action can
begin before impacts materialize, while Chile relies on
institutional capacity accumulated through sustained

investment in prevention and emergency response.
Mexico and Peru occupy an intermediate position. Both
can mobilize substantial resources, but the boundary
between adaptation, disaster recovery and broader
public spending remains blurred, limiting confidence
that current expenditure will reduce future losses. Bolivia
and Argentina appear more constrained for different
reasons: Bolivia’s response remains centered on relief
and dependent on the completion of financing approvals,

whereas Argentina’s emergency mobilization coexists
with a weakening of permanent wildfire-management
capacity. The comparison therefore exposes a central
regional fault line. Governments may respond forcefully
once a disaster occurs without necessarily building
resilience between events. Stronger adaptation requires
more than larger emergency packages: it depends
on protected multi-year budgets, clearly identified
adaptation spending, pre-agreed financing triggers

and institutions capable of preserving expertise and
preparedness from one season to the next.

North America
North America shows that adaptation responses can
differ as much within countries as between them,
shaped by the hazards faced and the strength of
existing prevention systems (Table 4). California
presents the strongest response in terms of financial
scale and structural prevention. Following the Los
Angeles wildfires, the state combined updated hazard
maps, expanded prescribed burning and fuel reduction
with support for home hardening, backed by USD4bn

for wildfire and forest resilience. Canada offers the
most developed community-based model. Its FireSmart
framework links local planning, risk assessment and
public preparedness, with dedicated funding for
Indigenous communities and public warning systems.
Implementation nevertheless remains uneven across
high-risk areas. Texas represents a more reactive
stage of adaptation. The Hill Country floods exposed
gaps in forecasting, warning systems and emergency

communication, prompting new investment in sirens,
gauges, meteorological infrastructure and disaster
response. These measures address clear operational
weaknesses, but place less emphasis on land-use
planning and long-term reduction of flood exposure. The
contrast reflects the nature and history of the hazards:
recurrent wildfire losses have encouraged California and
Canada to build permanent prevention frameworks,
while the Texas response followed a disaster that

revealed neglected local preparedness.

Sub-Saharan Africa
Recent climate events are driving two different stages
of adaptation in Sub Saharan Africa: anticipatory
support for slow moving drought and stronger
emergency management for sudden floods (Table
5). In Ethiopia, Kenya and Somalia, the drought
response reaches beyond humanitarian relief through
preparedness plans, cash support, solar-powered
irrigation, water access and livestock assistance. Its

design is increasingly preventive, but implementation
remains too limited for the scale of regional exposure.
The separately identified funding for East Africa and
Somalia also remains fragmented across programs.
Southern Africa has developed stronger operational
capacity for floods. Expanded forecasting, hydrological
monitoring and preventive evacuations helped countries
act earlier, while search-and-rescue operations and
drone assessments improved the immediate of response.

Yet repeated damage across Madagascar, Malawi,
Mozambique, South Africa, Zambia and Zimbabwe
shows that better warning cannot compensate for
vulnerable housing, transport and water infrastructure.
The difference largely reflects the hazards themselves:
drought develops gradually and allows earlier livelihood
protection, whereas floods require rapid warning and
evacuation. Across both regions, progress is most visible
in forecasting and emergency action, while investment

in resilient infrastructure and long-term risk reduction
remains less developed. Adaptation is therefore
advancing, but financing and coverage still fall short of
the geographic scale and recurrence of the shocks.

Iran; drought-resilient crops and

groundwater management;
national drought early-warning
and response planning[81]

remain limited relative to a crisis now
declared a national security threat[84]
USD 39mn Green Climate Fund

resilience[85]; no dedicated

budget identified
Middle East and North Africa (MENA)
MENA faces a dual hydrological risk: chronic
drought and water scarcity coexist with episodic but
potentially severe flooding, while preparedness and
policy maturity vary sharply across countries (Table
6). Morocco represents the clearest evidence that
sustained investment in early warning, hazard mapping
and disaster-risk institutions can reduce human impacts,
with large-scale preventive evacuations helping to

limit casualties during recent flooding. Tunisia has also
strengthened flood-risk management, but its response
remains more focused on post-event repairs, drainage
clearance and the scaling-up of protective infrastructure.
Drought adaptation is more challenging in Syria and
Iraq, where high exposure is compounded by structural
water scarcity, weaker institutional capacity and
broader socioeconomic vulnerability. Beyond domestic
adaptation, transboundary water cooperation is also

becoming increasingly important, as illustrated by Iraq’s
expanding water-management partnership with Turkey.
Overall, stronger preventive investment and cooperation
on shared water resources will be key to improving
regional resilience

September 14, 2026

IN THIS ISSUE
Macro Strategy—Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade:
Rapid advances in Artificial Intelligence (AI) have prompted prominent architects of
the new economy to conclude the Singularity is arriving ahead of schedule as signs
of its effects proliferate in the multitude of new AI models running in the increasing

number of data centers driving one of the biggest investment booms in U.S. history.
Superintelligence that surpasses collective human intelligence has long been one of the
predictions of futurists analyzing the implications of the Singularity. Superabundance is
one of the results of Superintelligence predicted by those who have written about the
consequences of this major acceleration in technological innovation. The early arrival of
these radical forces for change poses imminent challenges for society and investors that

will shape the next decade in critical ways.
In particular, the heightened uncertainty about how this new era plays out increases the
utility of a well-diversified approach to wealth management in our view.
Market View—Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite: The recent
rise in Treasury yields reflects a confluence of factors, including Federal Reserve (Fed)
policy uncertainty, deteriorating fiscal dynamics, resilient economic growth, persistent

inflation concerns and a record wave of debt issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate borrowers, particularly in the long end of the curve. Importantly, we view this
as a period of market adjustment rather than a cause for alarm.
Episodes of debt pressures can be a natural consequence of elevated supply, in addition
to other external factors and could be resolved through higher yields that help balance
supply and demand. While interest rate volatility may remain elevated and long-term

yields could settle at structurally higher levels, today's combination of attractive nominal
and real yields presents a potential opportunity for investors to extend beyond cash and
short-duration holdings and move closer to strategic duration targets.
The Ticker Tape—Top Questions Answered Directly by Our Chief Investment
Officer (CIO).
• What are the markets actually telling us?

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
While some academics have claimed the Singularity is still off in the distant future, or even
unlikely at all, those on the front lines of AI model development have recently asserted that it
has already begun. For example, Elon Musk has pointed to recent issues with AI models going

outside their proscribed limits, including hacking external systems and completing previously
unsolved math problems. Over a year ago, Sam Altman wrote, "We are past the event horizon; the
takeoff has started. Humanity is close to building superintelligence..." In short, the rapid evolution
of these models has convinced knowledgeable observers that the Singularity has begun.
Superintelligence is the primary driver of the Singularity as exponential advances in AI ultimately

create an artificial Superintelligence that is much smarter and faster than the collective
intelligence of humanity. As such, it can solve practical problems that remain unsolved by humans
and do it in a timely fashion. Superabundance is the logical implication of Superintelligence
as it works to create a world where economic goods, services and energy are essentially free
because of the omniscience implied by the most efficient processing of unlimited information by

superintelligent models. In essence, Superintelligence could mimic the Creation.
Some aspects of Superabundance include automated production of desired output that
eliminates most physical labor, clean energy and synthetic materials that eliminate resource
scarcity, and increased longevity as Superintelligence is applied to maintaining physical health
via, for example, synthetic organ replacement and personalized illness treatments. Obviously,
Superabundance pulls the rug out from under traditional economics, which is the study of how

scarce resources are allocated. While the "scarcity" vanishes in this new world, the allocation issue
remains and is likely to become the focus of political debate. In particular, the question of how
jobs and incomes fit into a superabundant world is likely to become a bigger and bigger issue.
The trend of technology replacing more and more of the labor share of income is already decades
old and dates back to the beginnings of digital technology. That trend has picked up speed

with the increasing profit share of output the new technologies created. The ratio of income
to the number of workers is orders of magnitude higher for the big new technology companies
compared to the old economy companies of the past. This means the owners of capital
(shareholders) are receiving a bigger share of the economic pie as high-margin, high-productivity
companies based in the new technology become a bigger part of the economy. This trend is

accelerating. That is a direct implication of the movement toward and through the Singularity.
It means the issue of income inequality, which has always been on society's radar screen, will
become bigger in the years ahead unless there are wise policies to share the superabundance
of the near future. Some countries are better prepared for this future than others. Norway,
for example, has built a sovereign wealth fund (SWF) over the past 25 years that represents a

substantial share of capital income for each of its citizens. In the U.S., with its widespread equity
culture, thanks to Individual Retirement Accounts and 401K plans, capital income is more widely
distributed compared to Europe with its much more debt-based capital markets. In any event,
widespread equity ownership is one way to deal with a superabundant economy where AI has
significantly reduced the labor share of income.
Demographics add to the natural evolution from labor income to capital income as societies

become wealthier. Aging societies of retirees by definition depend on capital income more than
labor income. This transition of the economy to higher margins and faster earnings growth is
the fortunate accompaniment to the need for more income from capital as societies become
older. The longevity trend naturally accelerates this process, which is the logical result of
technological progress raising productivity and living standards. People live longer in wealthy

societies, as subsistence constraints on life spans diminish while lifestyle and mental health
constraints become more the issue. How lifestyles and mental health evolve in a world where
work becomes less necessary is already showing signs of their increased importance for the
future of public health.
The current accelerating profits cycle reflects this impact of new technology on the relative
returns to labor and capital. The trend so far has been relatively slow compared to what's

coming if Superintelligence and Superabundance are the outcomes the Singularity promises as
increasingly seems likely. Because much financial analysis relies on past cycles the accelerating
Investment Implications
Rapid innovation, political upheaval
and increasing creative destruction
raise the difficulty of valuing
longer duration assets as we saw
earlier this year in the software
space. Higher-risk premia are a
likely result. On balance, this favors

Value-oriented assets over Growth.
Higher real yields in a strong capital
spending environment reinforce this
preference.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
profits and rising margins of the current economy have naturally focused attention on what
happens when conditions revert to the mean of lower margins and more historically normal
profit growth. Looked at through this lens, Equities appear overvalued and destined to correct
bubble-like conditions. The Singularity, however, implies "this time is different," the four most
dangerous words in investing. Yet, the slowly evolving shift toward capital income already under

way is an early warning sign of bigger things to come as these trends are turbo-charged to warp
speed while the economy passes through the Singularity. Seen this way, a super-cycle in profits,
margins and stock values seems logical.
A revamping of the social safety net to address the conditions implied by Superabundance
is already a hot topic in Silicon Valley. Various proposals for a universal basic income (UBI)
are being bandied about. Typically, UBI refers to the government giving everyone an income

sufficient to meet some living standard. This automatically implies a big role for government in
the new economy. Given the fourth-turning breakdown in the political process and governance,
alternatives that avoid the government sector might also be considered. For example, another
implication of the Singularity is an accelerating accumulation of wealth fueling a growing supply
of altruistic and philanthropic potential that arguably could be administered more efficiently by

the private sector than the government where widespread allegations of corrupt administration
at all levels have proliferated since the pandemic. Given the growing wealth pie and the diversity
of social priorities among its owners addressing more social needs through philanthropy is a
likely option for the superabundant economy. In any event, the relative roles of government and
the private sector are necessarily an issue to be hashed out in this revolutionary time.

More glimmers of the UBI-like movements have popped up recently. As mentioned, Norway's
SWF is an example of how a well-administered government has been preparing for this future.
In the U.S. government sponsored tax-advantaged savings accounts from birth have been
instituted with private donations being added to the program. Governments, including the U.S.,
have increasingly been buying stakes in private investments for various purposes with the result

that the focus solely on sovereign debt can be a misleading indicator of fiscal fragility.
Japan is a significant example of the difference between gross government debt and net
government debt after subtracting government owned assets. Its gross debt is around 279% of
its gross domestic product (GDP) which is much higher than any other major economy. However,
Japan government assets total around 192% of GDP , including domestic and foreign Equities,

bonds, and loans. As a result, its net debt is less than 100% of GDP and more in line with other
major economies. As one of the most aged societies in the world, Japan points the way toward
how societies can cope with longevity. Government ownership of a broad array of higher yielding
global assets funded with low sovereign debt interest rates appears to be a path the U.S.
Treasury is also starting to pursue in managing its debt burden.
The main risks to the uptopian promise of Superabundance are the dystopian possibilities

that would follow from poor social decision-making. Around the world, different governments
will follow different approaches to navigating the unprecedented challenges posed by the
Singularity. As the U.S. celebrates its 250-year anniversary, it is in the midst of its fourth "fourth
turning." These are the intense political upheaval periods that demographers and historians have
documented in every fourth generation. The Revolutionary War, the Civil War, and the Great

Depression and World War II mark the first three "fourth turnings." The Singularity is a major
marker for the current fourth turning.
The relative success of the U.S. in transitioning through the previous major turning points in
history provides hope for a utopian rather than a dystopian outcome. Other countries have not
transited these challenges so well for such an extended period which helps explain how the
U.S. has become the most prosperous country on earth. Nevertheless, past performance is no

guarantee of future success. As always, each generation faces new challenges to overcome, or not.
For investors, the Singularity seems to be behind an unusually strong earnings cycle that could
potentially last much longer than past history would suggest. That's positive for Equities and
risk assets in general. The extra risks associated with this rapid transformation argue for well
diversified holdings as creative destruction on steroids seems a plausible outcome in a world of

warp speed change.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite
Chief Investment Office, Fixed Income Strategy Team
For much of the past two decades, rising debt levels appeared to carry few
consequences. Federal deficits expanded and corporate borrowing increased, yet
Treasury yields generally trended lower and credit spreads stayed remarkably tame.
Today, the backdrop may be changing.

Federal borrowing needs continue to grow as deficit spending remains elevated. Net
interest expense has surpassed $1 trillion annually and now rivals total U.S. defense
spending. On this front there appears no end in sight as the Congressional Budget
Office projects that the fiscal 2026 shortfall will widen to roughly $2.1 trillion. Investors
are increasingly focused on not only the level of debt outstanding, but also the pace at
which new debt is being issued—with some questioning about the sustainability of this

situation over the longer term.
This phenomenon is not limited to Treasurys, as the issuance glut extends to corporate
debt. Investment-grade (IG) corporate issuance is running roughly 40% above last year
and is on pace for a record year. 1 While demand for Fixed Income remains healthy, the
sheer volume of new debt being brought to market is beginning to test investor capacity,
particularly at the long end of the Treasury curve and within certain sectors.

Exhibit 1: Low Yields Post-2008/2009 GFC* Have Fueled a Borrowing Binge.

Hyperscalers Are Competing for Long-Duration Capital. A significant driver of
the surge in corporate issuance has been the hyperscalers. The largest technology
companies are financing an unprecedented wave of AI-related capital spending, leading
to nearly $280 billion of debt issuance year-to-date. 2 Many of these issuers carry high
credit ratings and have ramped up issuance of longer-maturity bonds. As a result, they
are increasingly competing with long-dated Treasurys for the same pool of institutional

capital, including insurance companies, pension funds, sovereign wealth funds and
duration-oriented Fixed Income investors—creating a crowding-out effect amongst high-
quality Fixed Income.
Fortunately, this is a case of pressure, not a credit crisis. Recent weakness in long-dated
Treasurys reflects a classic supply-and-demand imbalance and importantly is not a
solvency crisis for the U.S. government or high-quality hyperscalers. Investors are being

asked to absorb a growing volume of net new issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate America at the same time.
Debt Pressure and a Normalization, Not a Fiscal Reckoning. The rise in long-end
yields during August reflects this dynamic. The 30-year Treasury yield moved above
5.3%, reaching levels not seen since 2007. Fiscal concerns likely played a role, but we

reflects a collision of fiscal co ncerns,

process, not a cri sis of confidence .

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
believe heavy supply, sticky inflation, reduced Fed demand and increased competition for
investor capital were equally important contributors.
And importantly, the market's adjustment mechanism is already working. Higher Treasury
yields and somewhat wider credit spreads are attracting buyers and helping clear the growing
supply of bonds. This is a natural normalization and is how markets restore equilibrium.

Recent Treasury auctions continue to attract demand consistent with historical norms, and
investor appetite for new IG issuance has remained robust. These are not characteristics
typically associated with a loss of confidence in U.S., or corporate, creditworthiness.
A New Yield Regime. For Fixed Income investors, the more important implication
is that a 4% to 5% 10-year Treasury yield may represent the new normal. In an
environment of very strong underlying economic growth and unprecedented volumes of

debt issuance from both the public and private sectors, higher yields may be required to
attract sufficient capital amid a more competitive landscape for yield.
We continue to view both nominal and real yields as attractive and believe investors
should remain focused on achieving strategic duration targets. While rate volatility may
persist, today's yield levels provide a potential opportunity to seek attractive income that
has been largely absent for much of the past decade.

The Bigger Picture: Growth Still Matters More Than Debt Levels. None of this dismisses
the long-term fiscal challenges facing the U.S. Debt levels are rising, interest costs
continue to grow, and mandatory spending increasingly consumes a larger share of the
federal budget. However, history shows that debt levels alone do not determine interest
rates. Between 2007 and 2020, U.S. debt rose dramatically even as the 10-year Treasury
yield fell from roughly 5% to below 1%. What ultimately matters is the interplay between

short-term interest rates, debt levels, economic growth, inflation and investor demand.
For instance, nominal GDP growth and the 10-year yield tend to be correlated, outside
of recessions—we believe recent rate increases partly reflect this strong macro
backdrop. Moreover, economic growth is running above long-end yields, supporting the
sustainability of rising government debt.

The U.S. continues to benefit from several structural advantages: the dollar remains
the world's reserve currency, Treasurys remain the foundation of the global financial

system, and U.S. capital markets are still the deepest and most liquid in the world. Those
advantages continue to support demand for U.S. assets and underpin sovereign credit
quality even as borrowing needs grow.
The bottom line, in our view, is that even though the recent rise in long-end yields catches
many headlines, there is no need to panic. Instead, the recent repricing reflects periodic
episodes of debt pressure rather than a fundamental loss of confidence in U.S. credit. With

borrowing demand from both Washington and corporate America likely to remain elevated,
bond prices may continue to serve as the market's release valve. The consequence is likely
a world of structurally higher long-term yields, not a sovereign debt crisis.

6 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What are the markets actually telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investmen t Officer
When the markets speak loudly we should listen. There are often clues in the internal trends
within the markets that help us determine how far into an economic cycle we are or what drivers
of growth are most impactful. In addition, we can also find clues that help with determining

the micro picture. In other words, what industries are the most likely to benefit, from a
profitability standpoint is concerned, as the growth picture unfolds? Currently, the markets have
a megaphone, in our view. The year-to-date sharp outperformers have been consistent and
should continue to advance. These include commodities, including copper and oil, Emerging
Markets, Japan in the Developed Markets, Small-cap relative to Large-cap shares, Value versus

Growth, and more cyclically-oriented companies versus more defensive. This current consistent
outperformance informs us that we are still early- to mid-cycle in a supply-led expansion. We are
still early in the build out of the supply that is needed for the natural demand that is there. New
infrastructure is needed to handle the new economy that is progressing and quickly innovating.
This infrastructure needs commodities, all forms of energy, battery storage, minerals, metals,

recycled water, gases, chemicals, and all the compute and cloud capacity, including memory
and other components to handle the path toward mass tokenization. Moreover, the buildup of
government debt in the advanced economies as fiscal deficits widen is forcing large institutional
investors (including central banks and other government entities) to diversify their reserve
bases. This has increased the interest in gold holdings. Countries that have a vast amount of

natural resources, especially oil and gas, are in high demand by investors, which is obvious when
examining year-to-date outperformers. The bottom line is that the markets are telling us that we
are still early- to mid-cycle in this massive buildout, which will require a wide collection of hard
assets, Infrastructure (old and new), land, and within Equities, a more diversified approach to
exposure—particularly areas that are more cyclical- and Value-oriented. We expect this view to

extend itself well into the foreseeable future if not years.
In Fixed Income, it may seem more complex, but it is actually easier to understand what the
markets are telling us, in our opinion. The backup in
yields is “real yield” driven, which is more due
to higher nominal growth than to concerns of long-run inflation. This is evident in the Treasury
inflation protection market, break-evens in general and the ratio of Treasury yields relative to
other Go

vernment bond markets in the developed world. However, rising oil and gas prices and
inflation gauges that are still above the preferred level are usually the excuses for yields backing
up. Deficit concerns are a more plausible reason f
or yields rising but the main driver, in our view,
is still abo
ve average nominal growth. The yield curve and rates in general are still adjusting to
this new economic landscape that is very different than those of the most recent past.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Bloomberg Commodity Index designed to provide liquid and diversified exposure to commodities via futures contracts. Is calculated on an excess return basis and reflects commodity
futures price movements.
MSCI Emerging Markets Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets countries. With 1,205 constituents, the index covers approximately 85% of the free
float adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI Japan Index is designed to measure the performance of the large and mid cap segments of the Japanese market.

MSCI Pacific ex Japan Index captures large and mid cap representation across 4 of 5 Developed Markets (DM) countries in the Pacific region (excluding Japan). With 93 constituents, the index
covers approximately 85% of the free float-adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI All-Country World Index is a premier benchmark representing global equity performance, covering large and mid-cap stocks across 23 developed and 24 emerging markets.

MSCI World Index ex U.S. captures large and mid cap representation across 22 of 23 Developed Markets countries--excluding the United States..
MSCI USA Momentum Index is based on MSCI USA Index, its parent index, which captures large and mid-cap stocks of the U.S. market. It is designed to reflect the performance of an equity
momentum strategy by emphasizing stocks with high price momentum, while maintaining reasonably high trading liquidity, investment capacity and moderate index turnover.

U.S. Large-cap Growth/Russell 1000 Growth Index measures the performance of the large-cap growth segment of the U.S. equity universe. It includes those Russell 1000 companies with
relatively higher price-to-book ratios, higher I/B/E/S forecast medium term (2 year) growth and higher sales per share historical growth (5 years).
U.S. Small-cap/Russell 2000 Index measures the performance of the small-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe.
MSCI USA High Dividend Yield Index is designed to reflect the performance of equities in the MSCI USA Index (excluding REIT s) with higher dividend income and quality characteristics than

average dividend yields that are both sustainable and persistent.
U.S. Mid Cap/Russell Midcap Index measures the performance of the mid-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe. The Russell Midcap Index is a subset of the Russell 1000® Index. It
includes approximately 800 of the smallest securities based on a combination of their market cap and current index membership.
MSCI USA Value Index captures large and mid-cap U.S. securities exhibiting overall value style characteristics.

MSCI USA Equal Weighted Index represents an alternative weighting scheme to its market cap weighted parent index, the MSCI USA Index. At each quarterly rebalance date, all index
constituents are weighted equally, effectively removing the influence of each constituent’s current price.
MSCI USA Minimum Volatility Index aims to reflect the performance characteristics of a minimum variance strategy applied to the large and mid-cap USA equity universe.

Investing involves risk, including the possible loss of principal. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

6 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.
S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index is an early monthly economic indicator that estimates overall private-sector business activity by combining manufacturing

or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and
investment advisory services, including the type of advice and assistance provided, the fees charged, and the rights and obligations of the parties. It is important to understand the differences,
particularly when determining which service or services to select. For more information about these services and their differences, speak with your Merrill financial advisor.

of Bank of America Corporation.

Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• Higher oil and refined fuels prices, a lower
exchange rate, higher long term interest rates
and weak house price trends justify a review of
our 2026 forecasts.
• We continue to see CPI inflation falling to 3.7%
y/y in the September quarter, but now think
inflation will rise to 4.2% in the December
quarter. Higher global fuel prices and a weaker
New Zealand dollar are driving these trends.
• Commodity export prices remain robust and

are reflected in a rise in our forecast for the
2026/27 Fonterra Milk Price to $9.75/kg. Our
opening bid for the Milk Price in 2027/28 is a
solid $9.50/kg.
• House prices have not stabilised as expected
in recent months. As a result, we have revised
down our 2026 house price forecast from a
small rise to a 1.4% decline.
• The combination of high fuel costs and a weak
housing market will likely crimp consumer
demand running into Christmas.
• We have modestly revised down our GDP

forecasts for the December quarter, which
reduces our 2026 growth forecast to a still
solid 2.5%. Risks appear skewed to the
downside given higher energy prices and long-
term interest rates.
• We will look at our 2027 forecasts in the
coming week.
• Our OCR forecasts remain unchanged. We see
a pause at the October meeting and a hike to
3% at the release of the December Monetary
Policy Statement.
Inflation – All gas, no brakes.
We’ve updated our near-term inflation forecasts in

response to recent global developments.
We continue to estimate that consumer prices rose by
0.7% in the September quarter, consistent with recent
monthly prices updates from Stats NZ. That would see
annual inflation ease to 3.7% in the year to September,
down from 4.1% in the year to June. We’ll confirm that
figure next week, when the final monthly prices update
for the September quarter is released on 16 October.

However, that easing in inflation appears set to be only
a temporary reprieve. We now expect consumer prices
to rise 1.1% in the December quarter. That would see
annual inflation rising to a three-year high of 4.2% by

the end of this year. The main driver of this revision are
sharply higher global fuel prices, compounded by a more
than 4% fall in the New Zealand dollar since the start of
September. Together, those developments have already
pushed local petrol prices up around 50 cents / litre over
the past month, with the nationwide average price of 91
unleaded reaching a record $3.53 / litre earlier this week.

RBNZ target range
Our updated year-end inflation forecast is above the
RBNZ’s September projection of 3.9%. In a recent
presentation, Governor Breman noted that the rise in oil
prices meant the risks to the RBNZ’s near-term inflation
forecast were to the upside. However, she also noted that

the RBNZ’s focus was on the medium-term outlook.
We haven’t changed our quarterly inflation forecasts for
2027 at this stage. However, the upward revision to our
forecast for the December quarter means annual inflation
is set to remain firm through most of 2027, consistent
with the RBNZ’s forecasts. We will review our 2027
forecasts for inflation and growth more comprehensively
next week when we will also review our financial forecasts
with the release of the Westpac Market Outlook.

A more tepid housing market.
We had expected the housing market to stabilise through
the second half of 2026. However, recent trends have
disappointed. House sales volumes continue to weaken,
and the median number of days required to sell a house
has lengthened to 48 days. Not surprisingly, therefore,

average house prices have continued to nudge lower
despite more robust conditions in those areas – mostly
in the South Island – that are most exposed to the well-
performing export sector. The latest indicators suggest
no imminent change in this trend.
Given these weak trends, our previous forecast for
essentially flat house prices through the September and
December quarters now looks too optimistic. We have
revised down our house price forecast for the 2026 year

from a rise of 0.6% y/y to a fall of 1.4% y/y on a national
basis. This is consistent with quarterly falls in house
prices of 0.5% and 0.8% q/q over the September and
December quarters.
The still weak labour market, higher mortgage rates and
still moderate employment growth all point to a weak
housing market for the foreseeable future. Risks remain
tilted towards another weak year in 2027. This would
especially be the case if increased taxation of capital

gains on investor housing were to occur as a result of the
outcome of the 2026 General Election.

It’s likely to be the case that there will be divergent trends
in the housing market across the country. Income and
employment trends in tourism and agriculture dominated

regions are expected to remain robust. Our upgrade to
the 2026/27 Milk Price released in our recent Agri Bites
report emphasises this point. But it looks like it will be
a while before investors and homeowners in the major
metropolitan areas in the North Island will be feeling
wealthier through a rising home value. On balance this
is likely to crimp consumer spending attitudes through
the end of 2026 and might moderate the recovery in the
construction sector that is underway.
